Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"I'm here because I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, sometimes staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked them, because if I don't do it one more time I feel like something bad will happen and it'll be my fault.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I guess I’m here because work has gotten kind of hard to manage. I do bookkeeping, and I keep finding myself going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over. Even after I’ve already checked them, I’ll feel like I need to do it just one more time.

It’s probably silly, but if I don’t, I get this really bad feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves. I know that sounds… excessive. I just haven’t been able to stop.

It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even after I know the numbers match, this thought keeps jumping in that I missed one hidden mistake in payroll or taxes and somebody's check won't go through or the firm will get in trouble, and I try to push it away but it comes right back like I can't trust my own eyes.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.

Like even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.

I’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.

I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s… pretty upsetting, honestly. I try to tell myself it’s just being careful, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really awful when I’m stuck there.

I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Um… no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or perfect alignment.

I mean, I do like things to look tidy, but I don’t sit there needing it to be perfectly lined up to feel okay. It’s more just wanting the information to be right, not so much how it looks.

Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves. I know that sounds… excessive. I mean… that’s where I notice it the most. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. I know that sounds bad. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center things because I’m still there checking work over and over.\n\nAnd at work, it’s affected things too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly a social invitation, but it’s all kind of tied to me not being able to stop and leave on time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or perfectly aligned for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or perfect alignment.\n\nI mean, I do like things to look tidy, but I don’t sit there needing it to be perfectly lined up to feel okay. It’s more just wanting the information to be right, not so much how it looks.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being a big thing for me. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any other areas of your life, besides work and the activities you've mentioned, that you feel are being negatively impacted by your need to repeatedly check things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting at work, and I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… mostly it’s been the same kinds of things I mentioned. Work has definitely been affected — like missing that filing deadline and having to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really hard.

And outside of work, it’s mainly that I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I just can’t get out of the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.

I don’t know if there are other areas I’m not thinking of, but those are the main ones I can point to. Is that what you mean?

Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves. I know that sounds… excessive. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.\n\nLike even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to let it, but it has been affecting things.\n\nWork-wise, I actually missed a filing deadline, which was really embarrassing, and I got called into a formal meeting about it. I know that sounds bad. I wasn’t trying to be careless or anything—I just got so stuck rechecking other things that I didn’t move on in time.\n\nAnd outside of work… I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some of my youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office when I’m supposed to. So… yeah, it causes a lot of distress. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had to turn down social invitations or avoid certain activities because of your need to repeatedly check your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, unfortunately.\n\nI try not to cancel on people if I can help it, but there have been times I just couldn’t get out of the office when I was supposed to. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center things because I’m still there checking work over and over.\n\nAnd at work, it’s affected things too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly a social invitation, but it’s all kind of tied to me not being able to stop and leave on time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or perfectly aligned for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or perfect alignment.\n\nI mean, I do like things to look tidy, but I don’t sit there needing it to be perfectly lined up to feel okay. It’s more just wanting the information to be right, not so much how it looks.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being a big thing for me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and the activities you've mentioned, that you feel are being negatively impacted by your need to repeatedly check things?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly it’s been the same kinds of things I mentioned. Work has definitely been affected — like missing that filing deadline and having to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really hard.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s mainly that I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I just can’t get out of the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.\n\nI don’t know if there are other areas I’m not thinking of, but those are the main ones I can point to. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you feeling overwhelmed by your need to repeatedly check things, to the point where it's interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to like?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting at work, and I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been getting in the way.

Like, I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. And I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I can’t seem to leave the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.

So… yes, I guess it is interfering with things I used to enjoy. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because work has gotten kind of hard to manage. I do bookkeeping, and I keep finding myself going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over. Even after I’ve already checked them, I’ll feel like I need to do it just one more time.\n\nIt’s probably silly, but if I don’t, I get this really bad feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves. I know that sounds… excessive. I just haven’t been able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the work stuff, honestly. I mean… that’s where I notice it the most. The spreadsheets, the calculations — I keep going back over them again and again, even when I know I already checked.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just one more time,” because if I don’t, I get this awful feeling like something bad might happen and it would be my fault. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.\n\nLike even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to let it, but it has been affecting things.\n\nWork-wise, I actually missed a filing deadline, which was really embarrassing, and I got called into a formal meeting about it. I know that sounds bad. I wasn’t trying to be careless or anything—I just got so stuck rechecking other things that I didn’t move on in time.\n\nAnd outside of work… I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some of my youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office when I’m supposed to. I keep telling myself it’s just this one time, but then it happens again. I feel really bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these worries and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting, honestly. I try to tell myself it’s just being careful, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really awful when I’m stuck there.\n\nLike, I’ll have already checked the spreadsheet or the calculation, and then I still feel this pressure to go back over it again—just one more time—because if I don’t, I get this feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. And I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nSometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t get myself to stop. So… yeah, it causes a lot of distress. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s too much detail.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall quality of life due to these checking behaviors and worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s definitely gotten in the way. I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was… really hard and embarrassing. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I just can’t seem to leave the office on time.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess my quality of life has been affected. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really, anyway.\n\nI mean, I like them to be neat and readable, but I don’t think there’s a certain number or pattern they have to follow for me to feel okay. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to turn down social invitations or avoid certain activities because of your need to repeatedly check your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, unfortunately.\n\nI try not to cancel on people if I can help it, but there have been times I just couldn’t get out of the office when I was supposed to. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because work has gotten kind of hard to manage. I do bookkeeping, and I keep finding myself going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over. Even after I’ve already checked them, I’ll feel like I need to do it just one more time.\n\nIt’s probably silly, but if I don’t, I get this really bad feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves. I know that sounds… excessive. I just haven’t been able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the work stuff, honestly. I mean… that’s where I notice it the most. The spreadsheets, the calculations — I keep going back over them again and again, even when I know I already checked.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just one more time,” because if I don’t, I get this awful feeling like something bad might happen and it would be my fault. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.\n\nLike even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to let it, but it has been affecting things.\n\nWork-wise, I actually missed a filing deadline, which was really embarrassing, and I got called into a formal meeting about it. I know that sounds bad. I wasn’t trying to be careless or anything—I just got so stuck rechecking other things that I didn’t move on in time.\n\nAnd outside of work… I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some of my youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office when I’m supposed to. I keep telling myself it’s just this one time, but then it happens again. I feel really bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these worries and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting, honestly. I try to tell myself it’s just being careful, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really awful when I’m stuck there.\n\nLike, I’ll have already checked the spreadsheet or the calculation, and then I still feel this pressure to go back over it again—just one more time—because if I don’t, I get this feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. And I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nSometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t get myself to stop. So… yeah, it causes a lot of distress. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s too much detail.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall quality of life due to these checking behaviors and worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s definitely gotten in the way. I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was… really hard and embarrassing. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I just can’t seem to leave the office on time.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess my quality of life has been affected. It’s more just… I want them to be correct, if that makes sense.\n\nBut if you mean like certain numbers feeling “right” or needing the columns to look a very specific way, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to turn down social invitations or avoid certain activities because of your need to repeatedly check your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, unfortunately.\n\nI try not to cancel on people if I can help it, but there have been times I just couldn’t get out of the office when I was supposed to. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center things because I’m still there checking work over and over.\n\nAnd at work, it’s affected things too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. Yeah… I mean, not exactly like it has to look perfect, maybe. It’s more that even when the numbers do match, I still get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake — like in payroll or taxes — and then someone’s check won’t go through, or the firm could get in trouble.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because work has gotten kind of hard to manage. I do bookkeeping, and I keep finding myself going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over. Even after I’ve already checked them, I’ll feel like I need to do it just one more time.\n\nIt’s probably silly, but if I don’t, I get this really bad feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves. I know that sounds… excessive. I just haven’t been able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the work stuff, honestly. I mean… that’s where I notice it the most. The spreadsheets, the calculations — I keep going back over them again and again, even when I know I already checked.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just one more time,” because if I don’t, I get this awful feeling like something bad might happen and it would be my fault. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.\n\nLike even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to let it, but it has been affecting things.\n\nWork-wise, I actually missed a filing deadline, which was really embarrassing, and I got called into a formal meeting about it. I know that sounds bad. I wasn’t trying to be careless or anything—I just got so stuck rechecking other things that I didn’t move on in time.\n\nAnd outside of work… I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some of my youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office when I’m supposed to. I keep telling myself it’s just this one time, but then it happens again. I feel really bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these worries and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting, honestly. I try to tell myself it’s just being careful, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really awful when I’m stuck there.\n\nLike, I’ll have already checked the spreadsheet or the calculation, and then I still feel this pressure to go back over it again—just one more time—because if I don’t, I get this feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. And I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nSometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t get myself to stop. So… yeah, it causes a lot of distress. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s too much detail.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall quality of life due to these checking behaviors and worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s definitely gotten in the way. I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was… really hard and embarrassing. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I just can’t seem to leave the office on time.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess my quality of life has been affected. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really, anyway.\n\nI mean, I like them to be neat and readable, but I don’t think there’s a certain number or pattern they have to follow for me to feel okay. It’s more just… I want them to be correct, if that makes sense.\n\nBut if you mean like certain numbers feeling “right” or needing the columns to look a very specific way, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to turn down social invitations or avoid certain activities because of your need to repeatedly check your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, unfortunately.\n\nI try not to cancel on people if I can help it, but there have been times I just couldn’t get out of the office when I was supposed to. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center things because I’m still there checking work over and over.\n\nAnd at work, it’s affected things too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly a social invitation, but it’s all kind of tied to me not being able to stop and leave on time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or perfectly aligned for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or perfect alignment.\n\nI mean, I do like things to look tidy, but I don’t sit there needing it to be perfectly lined up to feel okay. It’s more just wanting the information to be right, not so much how it looks.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being a big thing for me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and the activities you've mentioned, that you feel are being negatively impacted by your need to repeatedly check things?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly it’s been the same kinds of things I mentioned. Work has definitely been affected — like missing that filing deadline and having to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really hard.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s mainly that I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I just can’t get out of the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.\n\nI don’t know if there are other areas I’m not thinking of, but those are the main ones I can point to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your need to repeatedly check things, to the point where it's interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been getting in the way.\n\nLike, I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. And I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I can’t seem to leave the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is interfering with things I used to enjoy. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your need to check things repeatedly is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, honestly. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.\n\nLike even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to let it, but it has been affecting things.\n\nWork-wise, I actually missed a filing deadline, which was really embarrassing, and I got called into a formal meeting about it. I know that sounds bad. I wasn’t trying to be careless or anything—I just got so stuck rechecking other things that I didn’t move on in time.\n\nAnd outside of work… I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some of my youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office when I’m supposed to. I keep telling myself it’s just this one time, but then it happens again. I feel really bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these worries and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting, honestly. I try to tell myself it’s just being careful, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really awful when I’m stuck there.\n\nLike, I’ll have already checked the spreadsheet or the calculation, and then I still feel this pressure to go back over it again—just one more time—because if I don’t, I get this feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. And I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nSometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t get myself to stop. So… yeah, it causes a lot of distress. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s too much detail.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall quality of life due to these checking behaviors and worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s definitely gotten in the way. I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was… really hard and embarrassing. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I just can’t seem to leave the office on time.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess my quality of life has been affected. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really, anyway.\n\nI mean, I like them to be neat and readable, but I don’t think there’s a certain number or pattern they have to follow for me to feel okay. It’s more just… I want them to be correct, if that makes sense.\n\nBut if you mean like certain numbers feeling “right” or needing the columns to look a very specific way, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to turn down social invitations or avoid certain activities because of your need to repeatedly check your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, unfortunately.\n\nI try not to cancel on people if I can help it, but there have been times I just couldn’t get out of the office when I was supposed to. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center things because I’m still there checking work over and over.\n\nAnd at work, it’s affected things too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly a social invitation, but it’s all kind of tied to me not being able to stop and leave on time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or perfectly aligned for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or perfect alignment.\n\nI mean, I do like things to look tidy, but I don’t sit there needing it to be perfectly lined up to feel okay. It’s more just wanting the information to be right, not so much how it looks.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being a big thing for me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and the activities you've mentioned, that you feel are being negatively impacted by your need to repeatedly check things?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly it’s been the same kinds of things I mentioned. Work has definitely been affected — like missing that filing deadline and having to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really hard.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s mainly that I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I just can’t get out of the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.\n\nI don’t know if there are other areas I’m not thinking of, but those are the main ones I can point to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your need to repeatedly check things, to the point where it's interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been getting in the way.\n\nLike, I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. And I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I can’t seem to leave the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is interfering with things I used to enjoy. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your need to check things repeatedly is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s not just a little inconvenience anymore.\n\nI missed a filing deadline at work because I kept checking things, and then I got called into a formal meeting, which was pretty upsetting. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting my day-to-day life more than I’d like to admit. Is that what you mean by functioning?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the organization on the spreadsheets have to be exactly right, not just correct, for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like it has to look perfect, maybe. It’s more that even when the numbers do match, I still get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake — like in payroll or taxes — and then someone’s check won’t go through, or the firm could get in trouble.\n\nI try to tell myself I already checked it and it’s fine, but the thought comes right back, like I can’t really trust what I just saw. I know that probably sounds a little excessive… but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding mistakes than making everything perfect… at least that’s how it feels to me.\n\nEven when I know the numbers match, I get this thought that maybe I missed one hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes, and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away and tell myself I already looked, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a lot… but yeah, it’s more that fear of something being wrong.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.\n\nLike even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to let it, but it has been affecting things.\n\nWork-wise, I actually missed a filing deadline, which was really embarrassing, and I got called into a formal meeting about it. I know that sounds bad. I wasn’t trying to be careless or anything—I just got so stuck rechecking other things that I didn’t move on in time.\n\nAnd outside of work… I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some of my youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office when I’m supposed to. I keep telling myself it’s just this one time, but then it happens again. I feel really bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these worries and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting, honestly. I try to tell myself it’s just being careful, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really awful when I’m stuck there.\n\nLike, I’ll have already checked the spreadsheet or the calculation, and then I still feel this pressure to go back over it again—just one more time—because if I don’t, I get this feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. And I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nSometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t get myself to stop. So… yeah, it causes a lot of distress. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s too much detail.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall quality of life due to these checking behaviors and worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it’s definitely gotten in the way. I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was… really hard and embarrassing. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I just can’t seem to leave the office on time.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess my quality of life has been affected. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really, anyway.\n\nI mean, I like them to be neat and readable, but I don’t think there’s a certain number or pattern they have to follow for me to feel okay. It’s more just… I want them to be correct, if that makes sense.\n\nBut if you mean like certain numbers feeling “right” or needing the columns to look a very specific way, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to turn down social invitations or avoid certain activities because of your need to repeatedly check your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, unfortunately.\n\nI try not to cancel on people if I can help it, but there have been times I just couldn’t get out of the office when I was supposed to. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center things because I’m still there checking work over and over.\n\nAnd at work, it’s affected things too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly a social invitation, but it’s all kind of tied to me not being able to stop and leave on time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way things are organized on the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or perfectly aligned for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or perfect alignment.\n\nI mean, I do like things to look tidy, but I don’t sit there needing it to be perfectly lined up to feel okay. It’s more just wanting the information to be right, not so much how it looks.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being a big thing for me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and the activities you've mentioned, that you feel are being negatively impacted by your need to repeatedly check things?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly it’s been the same kinds of things I mentioned. Work has definitely been affected — like missing that filing deadline and having to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really hard.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s mainly that I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I just can’t get out of the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.\n\nI don’t know if there are other areas I’m not thinking of, but those are the main ones I can point to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your need to repeatedly check things, to the point where it's interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been getting in the way.\n\nLike, I missed a filing deadline at work and had to go into a formal meeting about it, which was really embarrassing. And I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I can’t seem to leave the office on time when I’m stuck checking things.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is interfering with things I used to enjoy. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your need to check things repeatedly is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s not just a little inconvenience anymore.\n\nI missed a filing deadline at work because I kept checking things, and then I got called into a formal meeting, which was pretty upsetting. And outside of work, I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and the youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting my day-to-day life more than I’d like to admit. It’s more that even when the numbers do match, I still get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake — like in payroll or taxes — and then someone’s check won’t go through, or the firm could get in trouble.\n\nI try to tell myself I already checked it and it’s fine, but the thought comes right back, like I can’t really trust what I just saw. I know that probably sounds a little excessive… but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding mistakes than making everything perfect… at least that’s how it feels to me.\n\nEven when I know the numbers match, I get this thought that maybe I missed one hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes, and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away and tell myself I already looked, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a lot… but yeah, it’s more that fear of something being wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is aimed at making sure everything is exactly as it should be, rather than just being correct?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, kind of. Not perfect like neat or pretty, but it has to feel exactly right, if that makes sense.\n\nEven after I know the numbers match, I get this thought that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked it, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s answering it the right way, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is driven by a need to make sure things are symmetrical or exact?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no… it’s not like I need the columns to look a certain way or be even.\n\nIt’s more that I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, even after I already checked them. Sometimes I stay after everyone else leaves, just to go through it one more time. It’s more about preventing mistakes, I think. Not perfection exactly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because work has gotten kind of hard to manage. I do bookkeeping, and I keep finding myself going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over. Even after I’ve already checked them, I’ll feel like I need to do it just one more time.\n\nIt’s probably silly, but if I don’t, I get this really bad feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves. I know that sounds… excessive. I just haven’t been able to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the work stuff, honestly. I mean… that’s where I notice it the most. The spreadsheets, the calculations — I keep going back over them again and again, even when I know I already checked.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just one more time,” because if I don’t, I get this awful feeling like something bad might happen and it would be my fault. I know that probably sounds a bit much… but sometimes I do end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but even after I can see the numbers match, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake, like in payroll or taxes.\n\nAnd then my mind kind of jumps to, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble, and it’s because I didn’t catch it. I try to tell myself it’s fine and push it away, but it comes right back. It’s like I can’t quite trust what I just saw with my own eyes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or order to feel right?\n\nPatient: Um… not exactly like a ritual, I guess. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep going back through the spreadsheets and calculations until it feels safe enough to stop.\n\nI’ll check them, and even if they look right, there’s this feeling like, “Just do it one more time.” And if I don’t, I worry something bad could happen and it would be my fault. So I end up repeating it… sometimes for hours after everyone else has left. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets and calculations is the only way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… in the moment, yes. I know it probably doesn’t make logical sense, because I’ve already checked them, sometimes more than once. But if I don’t go back over the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this really strong feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nSo it feels like checking is the only thing that makes it okay to stop… even though it doesn’t really stay okay for long. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s honestly why I end up staying so late sometimes, after everyone else has gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repeating thoughts or worries about things other than your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think it’s really other things, not in the same way. It’s mostly the work stuff—the numbers, payroll, taxes.\n\nLike even when I can see the numbers match, my brain still jumps to, “What if there’s one hidden mistake?” And then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm could get in trouble because I missed it. I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked, but it comes right back.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers the question exactly. It’s just… mostly that same kind of worry, like I can’t fully trust what I just saw.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to let it, but it has been affecting things.\n\nWork-wise, I actually missed a filing deadline, which was really embarrassing, and I got called into a formal meeting about it. I know that sounds bad. I wasn’t trying to be careless or anything—I just got so stuck rechecking other things that I didn’t move on in time.\n\nAnd outside of work… I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some of my youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office when I’m supposed to. I keep telling myself it’s just this one time, but then it happens again. I feel really bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these worries and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting, honestly. I try to tell myself it’s just being careful, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really awful when I’m stuck there.\n\nLike, I’ll have already checked the spreadsheet or the calculation, and then I still feel this pressure to go back over it again—just one more time—because if I don’t, I get this feeling that something bad could happen and it would be my fault. And I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nSometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t get myself to stop. So… yeah, it causes a lot of distress.
